feature_family: dramatic_case_setup
feature_id: feature_candidate_1285
feature_label: thesis_stated_in_opening_exchange
observation: "The dialogue opens with Hermogenes reporting Cratylus's thesis that names have correctness by nature, not by convention or agreement. The thesis is stated indirectly through Hermogenes, not by Cratylus himself, and is presented as the subject of a pre-existing conversation that Hermogenes now proposes to share with Socrates."
textual_basis: "At 383a-383b, Hermogenes reports Cratylus's claim that there is a correctness of name for each of the beings by nature, and that a name is not whatever people, having agreed, call a thing but that a certain correctness of names exists by nature, the same for Greeks and barbarians alike. The thesis is reported speech, not direct statement; Cratylus is present but silent during this opening exchange."
limits: "This observation identifies how the central thesis enters the dialogue and through which character. It does not evaluate whether Cratylus's view is endorsed or challenged, nor does it characterize the subsequent arguments."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "The dialogue's central definiendum — the correctness of names — is announced at 383a-383b but not yet defined. The passage sets up a nature-versus-convention opposition without specifying what correctness consists in. Cratylus's answer to Hermogenes's name challenge further dramatizes the gap between conventional naming and some standard of truth in names."
textual_basis: "At 383a-383b, Hermogenes reports Cratylus's claim that name-correctness exists by nature and is the same for Greeks and barbarians, rejecting the view that names arise by agreement. When Cratylus denies that Hermogenes's name is truly his, he invokes a standard — truth — without defining how that standard is to be applied. The term correctness (orthotes) is introduced but not explicated."
limits: "This observation notes that the definiendum is introduced and contested but not defined. It does not claim that Cratylus or Hermogenes has a correct or incorrect position, nor does it preview how the dialogue will (or will not) resolve the definitional question."

feature_label: socratic_aporetic_confession_solicited
observation: "Socrates asks Hermogenes a leading question about whether things have a stable being (bebaiotes tes ousias) of their own, and Hermogenes confesses that he has sometimes been carried away by aporia into the Protagorean position, though he does not really think it is correct."
textual_basis: "Socrates sets up a stark alternative at 385e-386a: either things have a private being for each person (Protagoras's view) or they have some stability of being of their own (bebaioteta tes ousias). Hermogenes at 386a admits that in his perplexity (aporon) he has sometimes been carried away (exenechthen) into the Protagorean view, but says it does not seem to him to be so (ou pany ti mentoi moi dokei houtos echein)."
limits: "This observation records the admission of aporia and the solicitation of a philosophical confession. It does not claim that Hermogenes holds the Protagorean view or that Socrates refutes it here."
review_status: accepted
```

The phrase echoes a contrast between subjective relativism and objective stable being that is characteristic of passages where Platonic forms are at stake."

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0015
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 386b-386c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt
  stephanus_span: 386b-386c
  start_marker: 386b
  end_marker: 386c
  start_char: 5027

  text_sha256: 457991e8a6ffd0f6d81777703ac68a937b9fea8f7706dbd29b00b7ba6c322020
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_1083
feature_label: refutation_by_counterexample
observation: "Socrates refutes Protagoras's 'man is the measure' thesis (referred to at 386c) by appealing to the acknowledged distinction between the wise (phronimoi) and the foolish (aphrones). He notes that if Protagoras were correct, no one would be wiser than anyone else, yet Hermogenes has just agreed that some people are very good (chrestoi) and wise, while others are very bad (poneroi) and foolish."
textual_basis: "At 386b Socrates elicits Hermogenes' assent that some people seem very good and wise, others very bad and foolish. At 386c he introduces the conditional: if Protagoras were right that things are as they seem to each person, then it would be impossible for some of us to be wise and others foolish. Hermogenes immediately denies this is possible, yielding a refutation of the Protagorean thesis in its universal form."
limits: "The observation records the logical structure of the refutation in the text. It does not assess whether Protagoras's actual doctrine (as opposed to its simplification here) is accurately represented, nor does it claim that the refutation is decisive."
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: "Socrates builds an assent chain in 386b-386c: he asks Hermogenes whether some people seem very wicked, then whether some seem very good, then how many (very few), then confirms the appearance holds, then introduces the equivalence of 'very good' with 'very wise' and 'very wicked' with 'very foolish.' Each step secures a small concession before the Protagorean thesis is introduced and rejected."
textual_basis: "The sequence at 386b-386c proceeds through five short exchanges: (1) Do some seem very wicked? Yes. (2) Do some seem very good? Yes, very few. (3) They do seem so? Yes. (4) Are the very good very wise and the very wicked very foolish? Yes. (5) If Protagoras were right, could some be wise and others foolish? No. The stepwise assent construction is explicit in the text."
limits: "This observation characterizes the formal structure of the exchange. It does not evaluate whether the equivalence of 'very good' and 'very wise' is justified in the argument."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates leads Hermogenes step-by-step through a series of short questions with minimal assents (385c-385d): whether the parts of a true logos must be true, whether smallest parts are true too, whether onoma is the smallest part of logos, and whether the part of a false logos must be false. Hermogenes assents with nai, phēmi, and pōs gar ou, culminating in agreement that a name can be called true or false."
textual_basis: "The exchange at 385c-385d consists of a tight sequence of yes/no questions from Socrates and brief affirmations from Hermogenes, compressing a multi-step inference (whole-truth implies part-truth; name is smallest part; therefore name inherits truth/falsity) into a rapid assent chain typical of Socratic elenchus."
limits: "This observation does not evaluate whether the premises are sound or whether Hermogenes could have resisted any step. It notes only the structural pattern of the exchange."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0019
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 385c-385d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt
  stephanus_span: 385c-385d
  start_marker: 385c
  end_marker: 385d
  start_char: 3440
  end_char: 4319
  text_sha256: b4883378ae51557de3d68b98a3844a622afb305852ca4ed72b42e986e82f5f04
greek_terms: ["μόρια", "σμικρότατον", "ὄνομα", "λόγου"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_522
feature_label: definition_established_by_assent_chain
observation: "Through an assent chain (385c-385d), Socrates establishes an operational definition: a name (onoma) is the smallest part (smikrotaton morion) of a logos, and because the whole logos is true or false, its smallest part — the name — must likewise be true or false. This definitional move attributes truth-evaluability to individual names through a whole-to-part property transfer."
textual_basis: "At 385c Socrates asks whether there is any smaller part of logos than onoma. Hermogenes answers 'no, this is the smallest' (ouk, alla touto smikrotaton). Socrates then infers that if a logos is true, its name-part is true, and if a logos is false, its name-part is false (385d). The conclusion 'it is possible to say a name is false or true' (estin ara onoma pseudos kai alethes legein) follows from the assent chain."
limits: "This observation notes the definitional structure of the argument. It does not endorse the inference that whole-truth entails part-truth, nor does it assess whether individual names are in fact truth-evaluable outside this argumentative frame."
review_status: accepted
```

greek_terms: ["ὀνόματος ὀρθότητα", "ἐμοὶ μὲν ἕτερον", "σοὶ δὲ ἕτερον", "ὃ ἐγὼ ἐθέμην"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_490
feature_label: definition_proposed
observation: "At 385d Hermogenes states his thesis about the correctness of names (onomatos orthotēs): each individual's name for a thing is correct for that individual. He claims he has no other criterion for name-correctness than this: 'I call each thing by a different name, the one I posited (ethēmen), and you by another, the one you posited.' This explicitly formulates the conventionalist position that naming correctness is private and relative to each name-giver."
textual_basis: "Hermogenes' own words at 385d: 'I myself have no other correctness of a name than this — that it is possible for me to call each thing by one name, which I posited, and for you by another, which you in turn posited' (ouk gar echō egōge… onomatos allēn orthotēta ē tautēn). This is offered as a positive thesis immediately after Socrates secures agreement that names can be true or false."
limits: "This observation records Hermogenes' statement of his position. It does not evaluate whether the conventionalist thesis is tenable, nor does it anticipate Socrates' subsequent examination of it."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: natural_specialization_principle
observation: "Socrates argues that actions are performed according to their own nature, not according to opinion, and illustrates this with cutting: success depends on cutting each thing according to the nature of cutting and being cut, using the tool by which it is naturally done; doing it contrary to nature results in error and accomplishing nothing."
textual_basis: "At 387a, Socrates states that actions are done according to their own nature, not according to human opinion. He then develops a cutting analogy in which the correct procedure is determined by the nature of the action and the instrument, and acting contrary to nature produces error and nothing accomplished. At 387b he extends the pattern to burning and then generalizes to all cases."
limits: "This observation records that the natural-specialization principle is announced and illustrated with craft-like actions. It does not assess whether the analogy to naming holds, nor does it evaluate the metaphysical claim that actions have natures independent of convention."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates introduces a criterion that distinguishes correct action from erroneous action: the correct way is that by which each thing naturally performs and undergoes the action, and with the instrument by which it naturally does so. The wrong way is anything done according to arbitrary opinion rather than according to nature."
textual_basis: "At 387b, Socrates contrasts acting according to every opinion with acting according to the correct opinion, and defines the correct one as that by which each thing naturally burns and is burned and with which it naturally does so. This formalizes a nature-based criterion for evaluating action correctness, set against opinion as the arbitrary alternative."
limits: "This observation records the articulation of a nature-based evaluative criterion. It does not assess whether the criterion is subsequently applied consistently or whether it is adequate for the domain of naming."

Hermogenes assents, forwarding the prior agreed premises into the new domain of naming.
textual_basis: At 387d Socrates posits the natural naming rule as a consequence of the earlier agreement about actions having their own nature, explicitly securing the move with a forward-reference to prior agreements.
limits: This observation records the structural use of forward-referenced assent to extend an argument to a new domain. It does not claim that the prior agreement was correct or that Hermogenes understood the implications.

textual_basis: At 389b, Socrates asks whether a shuttle-maker whose shuttle breaks will make another looking at the broken one or at the form according to which he made the broken one. Hermogenes agrees it is the form. Socrates then says this is what we would most justly call what a shuttle is.

textual_basis: At 389c, Socrates states the same principle applies to other tools. One must discover the naturally suited tool and render it, not as one wishes but as it naturally grows. He gives examples: the naturally suited drill must be set into iron, and the naturally suited shuttle into wood.
limits: The passage applies the principle to material tools such as shuttles and drills. It does not yet extend it to names or immaterial objects, though the context of the Cratylus suggests such an extension.

  text_sha256: 32a5a455e9320386e42f38db2bece1a9296ef294ad78ec7d1c33f8ebd91c0f5b
greek_terms:
  - φύσει ὀρθότητα ὀνόματος
english_gloss: "natural correctness of names"
feature_family: dramatic_case_setup
feature_id: feature_candidate_1856
feature_label: definiendum_marked_for_inquiry
observation: |
  Hermogenes requests that Socrates demonstrate the nature-given correctness of name (ten phusei orthoteta onomatos), and Socrates restates this as the next joint inquiry target: the correctness (orthotes) of name. The segment thus establishes the dialogue's core definiendum as an explicit item to be sought (zetein).
textual_basis: |
  At 391a Hermogenes asks Socrates to show what the natural correctness of a name is. Socrates at 391b converts this into a procedural question: "the next thing must be sought, if you desire to know, what in turn its correctness is." Hermogenes confirms his desire to know.
limits: |
  This observation records that the definiendum is explicitly marked for inquiry; it does not assess whether the inquiry succeeds or what a natural-name theory entails.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  text_sha256: e808f3b73a4e1429dcacaf8da1c6684441d5dbe990445e63a0b80b2083c262e4
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_1868
feature_label: craft_expertise_requires_supervisor
observation: Socrates constructs a two-role craft model for name-making: the name-giver (nomothetes) performs the making, while a dialectician (dialektikos) supervises the work, just as a helmsman supervises a carpenter making a rudder.
textual_basis: At 390d Socrates states that a carpenter makes a rudder under the supervision of a helmsman (epistatountos kybernetou), then at 390d he applies the same structure to names: the lawgiver makes names with a dialectician as supervisor (epistaten echontos dialektikon andra). The parallel is explicit in the repeated epistates/epistaton vocabulary.
limits: This observation records the presence of a two-role craft analogy. It does not claim that the analogy succeeds as an argument, nor does it evaluate whether the dialectician role is independently justified.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 390e Socrates says the name-maker must look to "the name that is by nature for each thing" (to tei physei onoma on hekastoi) and be able to place "its eidos" (autou to eidos) into letters and syllables. The language of eidos and looking-to (apobleponta) echoes Form-oriented vocabulary found in other dialogues.
limits: This observation notes the presence of eidos in a craft-production frame. It does not claim that a full theory of Forms is operative here, nor does it assess whether "eidos of a name" is coherent within the dialogue's own argument.

observation: Socrates cites Homer as an authority on the correctness of names, specifically invoking passages where Homer distinguishes (diorizei) between what humans call certain things and what the gods call them. Socrates argues that the gods' names are the ones that are correct by nature (physei onomata).
textual_basis: At 391d Socrates tells Hermogenes to learn from Homer and the other poets about names, then points to Homeric passages where divine and human names for the same things diverge. He states that the gods call things by the names that are by nature correct (pros orthotēta haper esti physei onomata).
limits: This observation records that Homer is cited as an authority within an argument about natural name correctness. It does not assess whether the Homeric citation is accurate or whether Socrates endorses the claim without qualification.

textual_basis: At 391c Hermogenes responds that his request would be strange (atopos) since he does not accept Protagoras's truth at all, yet would be cherishing things said according to that truth as if they were worth something.
limits: This records a respondent's rejection of a named authority's doctrine in an inquiry context. It does not evaluate whether Hermogenes' position is consistent or whether his characterization of Protagoras's truth is accurate.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0051
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 391e-392a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt

  end_char: 16320
  text_sha256: "280531a2f07da3a300d7e0b0f7c960ecc12b1f1bbef718e40d039e21de265cec"
greek_terms:
  - Ξάνθον
  - Σκάμανδρον
  - χαλκίδα
  - κύμινδιν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_1972
feature_label: divine_vs_human_naming
observation: |
  Socrates invokes a traditional formula distinguishing divine names from human names for the same entity. He quotes Homer: the gods call the river at Troy Xanthos, while men call it Skamandros; likewise a bird is called chalkis by gods, kymindis by men.
textual_basis: |
  At 391e Socrates cites the Homeric verse: "whom Xanthos the gods call, but men Skamandros." At 392a he extends the pattern to the bird chalkis/kymindis and mentions Batieia and Myrine as a further instance. The divine-human naming schema is presented as a recurring Homeric formula, not as Socrates' own claim.
limits: |
  The observation records the citation of a traditional Homeric motif. It does not settle whether Socrates endorses it literally or uses it as a heuristic for the correctness-of-names inquiry. The passage does not yet develop an argument about divine vs. human naming priority.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0052
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 391e-392a
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt
  stephanus_span: 391e-392a
  start_marker: "391e"
  end_marker: "392a"
  start_char: 15690
  end_char: 16320
  text_sha256: "280531a2f07da3a300d7e0b0f7c960ecc12b1f1bbef718e40d039e21de265cec"
greek_terms:
  - σεμνόν
  - φαῦλον
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_260
feature_label: mock_deference
observation: |
  Socrates frames knowledge of divine-versus-human naming as a matter that could be held noble (semnos) or trivial (phaulos). He asks Hermogenes whether he would consider the inquiry into why a river is called Xanthos rather than Skamandros a contemptible (phaulon) topic, inviting either serious or playful engagement.
textual_basis: |
  At 392a Socrates says: "do you not think this is something august (semnon) to know, in what way it is correct to call that river Xanthos rather than Skamandros?" He adds: "or, if you wish, the bird... do you think it a trivial (phaulon) matter of learning how much more correct it is to call the same bird chalkis rather than kymindis?" The alternative between august and trivial creates an ironic invitation to take the naming question seriously.
limits: |
  This is a procedural invitation, not a substantive argument about divine or human names. The irony is marked by the option to treat the topic as weighty or slight; the observation does not claim Socrates is mocking Hermogenes.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: |
  Socrates supports an etymological-naming inquiry by quoting two Homeric passages that juxtapose divine and human nomenclature. The Homeric verses are deployed as data for the correctness-of-names investigation rather than as mythological illustrations.
textual_basis: |
  At 391e Socrates quotes: "whom Xanthos the gods call, but men Skamandros." At 392a he quotes: "chalkis the gods call, but men kymindis." He also mentions the Homeric pair Batieia/Myrine. The Homeric authority is the sole evidential base for the divine-human naming distinction in this passage, and it is offered in response to Hermogenes' assent that whoever calls things by their divine name calls correctly (391e).
limits: |

review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: >-

  Astyanax while the women call him Scamandrius, and that Homer considered the
  men wiser. At 392e, the etymology is grounded in the Homeric claim that
  Hector alone defended the city, making it correct to call his son Astyanax

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0057
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 393a-393b
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt
  stephanus_span: 393a-393b
  start_marker: 393a

  text_sha256: b5522a4c95009eccb0fecd823e71db5aa3203be4e7b5351281e3a042155f81a8
greek_terms:
  - ἄναξ
  - Ἕκτωρ
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: etymology_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_1984
feature_label: name_etymology_via_semantic_equivalence
observation: Socrates argues that the names Hektor and anax are nearly the same in meaning, both being royal names, because wherever one is an anax, he is also a Hektor of that place.
textual_basis: Socrates explicitly says that anax and Hektor mean nearly the same thing (schedon ti tauton semainei) and that both names are royal (basilika). He then explains the reasoning: where someone is an anax, he is also a Hektor of that place, glossing Hektor through kratei, kektetai, and echei at 393b.
limits: This observation records the etymological claim made in the dialogue; it does not assess whether the etymology is linguistically correct or whether Plato endorses it.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: homeric_character_characterized
observation: "Socrates extracts from Homer's naming of Hector's son two names, Scamandrius and Astyanax, and treats the poet as implicitly favoring one name over the other as more correct (orthoteron) for the child, thereby treating a Homeric character's naming as a test case for name-correctness."
textual_basis: "At 392b Socrates sets aside broader Homeric examples as too large for him and Hermogenes, then proposes a more manageable human-level case: the two names Homer gives Hector's son. At 392b-392c he asks Hermogenes to judge which name Homer considers more correct, framing the poet as an authority on naming."
limits: "Does not establish that Homer himself had a theory of name-correctness; Socrates' inference about Homer's view is a dramatic device within the dialogue. The observation does not address the etymology of either name, which comes later."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: assent_chain
observation: "Socrates leads Hermogenes through a two-step assent chain to establish that the more intelligent (phronimoteroi) assign names more correctly and that men as a class are more intelligent than women, creating a premise by which Homer's naming preference can be aligned with the view of the more intelligent class."
textual_basis: "At 392c Socrates asks whether the more intelligent or the less intelligent assign names more correctly; Hermogenes agrees it is the more intelligent. Socrates then asks whether women or men are more intelligent (as a whole class); Hermogenes answers men. The chain builds premises that will anchor the subsequent argument about Homer naming Hector's son."
limits: "The observation records the structural assent chain, not a claim about Socratic or Platonic views on gender. The premise about men's greater intelligence is a dramatically reported opinion of Hermogenes, not an established Platonic doctrine."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Socrates introduces the criterion that a name is correct as long as the essence of the thing is made clear in the name, regardless of variations in syllables or the addition or removal of letters. He then illustrates this with the example of the letters, noting that most are referred to by names rather than the letters themselves, except for four (E, Y, O, Omega).
textual_basis: At 393c-393d Socrates states that differences in syllables or added/removed letters matter not at all, as long as the essence of the thing is made manifest in the name. He then begins to explain using the example of letters and their names.
limits: This observation registers the criterion as stated. It does not assess whether the criterion successfully resolves the problem of name correctness, nor does it evaluate the letter-name illustration that Socrates begins at the end of 393d.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

feature_id: feature_candidate_2079
feature_label: dynamis_distinct_from_letters
observation: "At 394b-394c, Socrates claims that names can differ completely in their letters and syllables while sharing the same power (dynamis). He illustrates this with names that signify 'king' (Astyanax, Hector, Archepolis), 'general' (Agis, Polemarchus, Eupolemus), and 'doctor' (Iatrocles, Acesimbrotus), noting they share few or no letters yet signify the same thing."
textual_basis: "At 394b, the name-expert is said not to be startled if a letter is added, removed, or transposed, or if the power of the name resides in entirely different letters. At 394c, the claim is generalized: many names may disagree in syllables and letters but express the same power (tei dynamei t'auton phthengomena). The examples Astyanax/Hector (sharing only tau) and the king/general/doctor name-groups provide supporting instances."
limits: "This observation records the claim as stated. It does not assess whether the argument succeeds, whether Socrates ultimately accepts this view, or how it relates to the dialogue's later naturalism/conventionalism debate."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 394c, Socrates groups proper names by what they signify rather than by shared letters. He lists names that signify 'king' (Astyanax, Hector, Archepolis), names that signify 'general' (Agis, Polemarchus, Eupolemus), and names that signify medical matters (Iatrocles, Acesimbrotus). The groups are introduced with the formula 'other names signify X' (alla ... semainei)."
textual_basis: "The classification proceeds in three explicit semantic categories at 394c: (1) king-names introduced with 'many other names signify nothing other than king' (alla polla estin ha ouden all' e basilea semainei), (2) general-names introduced with 'and others again signify general' (kai alla ge au strategon), (3) medical names introduced with 'and other medical ones' (kai iatrika ge hetera). Each category is followed by two or three proper-name examples."
limits: "This observation records the grouping structure. It does not claim that this taxonomy is exhaustive or that Socrates is proposing a systematic science of name classification."
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 394a, Socrates states that one may vary names with syllables so that to the layman they appear different, then compares this to doctors' drugs made different in appearance by colors and scents while being the same substance.

textual_basis: At 395c-395d, Socrates states that the name Pelops is fittingly given (emmetros keisthai), then explains that it signifies 'the one seeing what is near' (ton ta engus horonta) and retells the myth of Myrtilus's murder to illustrate that Pelops saw only the immediate (to engus monon) and not distant consequences.
limits: This observation records an etymological derivation offered by a speaker within the dialogue; it does not endorse the etymology as linguistically genuine or as Plato's own view.

observation: "Socrates applies the name-correctness principle he has just refined to three proper names: Theophilus, Mnesitheus, and Orestes. He then cites Orestes as a name that correctly indicates the bearer's beastly, wild, and mountainous nature."
textual_basis: "At 394e Socrates says the impious offspring of a pious parent should not be called Theophilus or Mnesitheus or any such name, because these signify the opposite (tanantia toutois semanei) if names are to attain correctness (tes orthotetos tunchanei ta onomata). He then proffers Orestes as a name that correctly indicates (endeiknumenos) the beastly, wild, and mountainous nature of its bearer."
limits: "This observation records the application of the genus-based naming rule to three specific proper names as test cases. It does not evaluate the etymological reasoning or assess whether the examples are persuasive."

feature_label: name_meaning_deliberately_veiled
observation: Socrates claims that the name Atreus is slightly deflected (paraklinei) and veiled (epikekalyptai) so that it does not reveal the man's nature to everyone, but only to those with expertise in names.
textual_basis: At 395b, Socrates states that the name's form is slightly deflected and veiled (smikron paraklinei kai epikekalyptai) so as not to make the man's nature clear to all; to those who understand names (tois d' epaiousi peri onomaton) it sufficiently reveals what Atreus means.
limits: This records the explicit claim about one proper name being deliberately obscured; it does not assess whether this veiling thesis applies to all names in the Cratylus or whether it represents Socrates' own view versus a hypothesis under examination.
review_status: accepted
```

  text_sha256: 3167a8773aaa2be96e59738800fffb397e43d5f123da5a1aaad53c2187de0bd9
greek_terms:
  - Διὸς
  - Ζῆνα
  - Δία
  - ζῆν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: etymology_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_1984
feature_label: name_etymology_via_semantic_equivalence
observation: Socrates analyzes the name of Zeus (Dios) by dividing it into its two dialectal variant forms and recombining them to reveal that the name signifies the god's nature as cause of life (zen) and ruler of all things.
textual_basis: At 396a Socrates states the name of Zeus is like a logos (statement). He divides it in two (dichelontes de auto dichēi), noting that people use one part (Zena) or the other (Dia). When combined (syntithemena) into one, the name reveals the nature (phusin) of the god. Socrates then identifies Zeus as the cause (aitios) of living (zen) and the ruler and king of all, showing the semantic basis of the etymology.
limits: This observation records the etymological procedure of splitting and recombining a name to extract semantic content. It does not evaluate the historical-linguistic validity of the etymology or Plato's attitude toward it.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: name_etymology_via_concealment
observation: The name Tantalus is explained as a deliberately concealed form of talantatos (most weighed-down or miserable), as though someone wishing to call him talantaton hid the word (apokruptomenos) and said Tantalon instead.
textual_basis: At 395e the speaker describes the name Tantalus as if someone who wanted to name him talantaton concealed it (apokruptomenos) and said Tantalon instead, and remarks that chance of report (he tuche tes phemes) seems to have furnished this kind of name for him.
limits: This observation records the concealment mechanism in name-etymology. It does not claim that this is the actual historical origin of the name or that Plato endorses the etymology.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Socrates states a normative principle about the function of a name: a name ought to be capable of revealing the nature (phusis) of its bearer.
textual_basis: At 396a, after the etymological analysis of Zeus, Socrates says that the combined name reveals the nature of the god, "which we say it is fitting (prosekein) for a name to be able to accomplish (apergazesthai)." This is a general claim about what names properly do.
limits: This observation records the explicit normative claim about the function of names. It does not assess whether this principle is consistently applied throughout the Cratylus or whether Plato endorses it.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 396b-c Socrates reinterprets Cronos through etymology as signifying the pure and unmixed of mind, traces Cronos to Ouranos and derives Ouranos from upward vision (seeing things above), and at 396c invokes Hesiod's genealogy as a source for names of earlier ancestors.
limits: This records the use of divine genealogy as a framework for etymological argument; it does not evaluate the correctness of the etymologies or Socrates' attitude toward Hesiodic authority.

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: etymology_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_1984
feature_label: name_etymology_via_semantic_equivalence
observation: "Socrates transitions from proper names whose correctness is uncertain to those that most likely exhibit correctness, identifying names concerning the eternal and naturally existing things as the most serious domain for name-setting. He asks whether it is not just to begin with the gods, investigating how the name theoi was correctly assigned, then offers his suspicion about its derivation."
textual_basis: "At 397b Socrates sets aside wish-based personal names like Theophilos and pivots to 'the things that always are and have being by nature' as the domain where name-setting was most earnestly pursued. At 397c he declares it proper to begin from the gods and states a suspicion (hypopteuo) about how the name theoi was correctly assigned, initiating an etymological investigation."
limits: "The observation records thematic shift and procedural framing, not the etymological content of the derivation itself, which unfolds in the subsequent lines. It treats the name-correctness priority claim as a text-internal methodological move, not as a philosophical thesis about language."
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: ""
feature_family: irony_marker
feature_id: feature_candidate_2198
feature_label: mock_inspiration_claim
observation: Socrates attributes his sudden facility with etymological analysis to daimonic inspiration absorbed from Euthyphro, then proposes a purification ritual for the following day to expel it. The claim is framed as a possession that overtook him involuntarily and will require a ritual specialist to remove.
textual_basis: Socrates says the inspiration "has just fallen upon me from I know not where" (396d) and that he blames Euthyphro of Prospalta for it. Hermogenes explicitly compares Socrates to those who are entheous (possessed/inspired) suddenly prophesying (396d). Socrates then proposes that they use the inspiration today but tomorrow perform an apodiopompetic purification through a priest or sophist (396e-397a).
limits: This observation does not determine whether Socrates is sincere about the possession or whether the etymology section that follows is meant to be taken seriously. It records only that the text stages the inspiration as an ironic external attribution with a purification frame.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: At 396e Socrates says "today we should use it and investigate the rest concerning names, and tomorrow, if you also agree, we will send it away (apodiopompēsometha) and purify ourselves (katharoumetha) having found someone skilled in such purifications, whether a priest or a sophist" (396e-397a). Hermogenes agrees at 397a.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0092
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 400c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt
  stephanus_span: 400c

  end_char: 33845
  text_sha256: 65317082149ade60c3706fa73cbc77e7e398e7e7be90b7a7fe30da69d62810a0
greek_terms:
  - σῆμα
  - σῶμα
english_gloss: "tomb/body wordplay"
feature_family: etymology_play
feature_id: feature_candidate_2288
feature_label: competing_etymologies_same_name
observation: "Socrates reports multiple competing etymologies for soma (body). Some say it is the sema (tomb) of the soul, as if the soul is buried in the present life. Others say it is correctly called sema because through it the soul signifies whatever it signifies. A third, Orphic derivation treats the body as a prison-enclosure in which the soul is kept until it pays its debts."
textual_basis: "At 400c Socrates introduces the first etymology with 'some say' (tines phasin), the second with the verb semainei, then offers an Orphic account as the one he finds most persuasive. He reports the Orphic etymology with dokein moi malista. Three distinct etymological accounts are offered for the same name within a single speech."
limits: "Socrates does not definitively settle on one etymology; he only expresses that the Orphic one seems most plausible to him. The observation records the presence of multiple competing etymologies presented for the same name, not a doctrinal claim about the body's nature."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0093
source_work: Cratylus

greek_terms:
  - Διός
english_gloss: "Zeus / knowledge of divine names"
feature_family: frame_depth
feature_id: feature_candidate_1012
feature_label: piety_constraint_on_logos
observation: "When Hermogenes asks whether they can investigate the correctness of the gods' names (such as Dios) in the same manner, Socrates responds that the finest method would be to acknowledge that they know nothing about the gods or the names the gods call themselves, since the gods surely call themselves by true names."
textual_basis: "At 400d, Hermogenes proposes extending the etymological method to divine names, mentioning Dios. Socrates replies with a piety-based restriction: one method is to say we know nothing about the gods or the names they use for themselves (ouden ismen), because the gods call themselves by true names (talethe kalousi)."
limits: "Socrates does not refuse to investigate divine names outright; he describes this piety-based approach as 'one method, the finest' (hena men ton kalliston tropon), implying other methods may also be possible. This record does not claim a blanket prohibition on divine etymology."
review_status: accepted
```

  names, restricting inquiry to the human name-givers' beliefs about the gods
  (401a). He frames this as a prayer-like preliminary declaration modeled on
  the customary practice, at 400e-401a, of addressing gods by the names they
  prefer.
textual_basis: |
  At 400e-401a the second mode of correctness is said to follow the custom
  (nomos) in prayers of calling the gods by whatever names they favor. At 401a
  Socrates says they will announce to the gods that they will investigate not
  the gods themselves but what opinion (doxa) humans held when assigning names
  to them; this, he says, is not blameworthy (anemeseton).
limits: |
  The observation records the procedural demarcation of a piety boundary
  within an etymological dialogue; it does not assert that Socrates or Plato
  holds any particular theological view.
review_status: accepted
```

  tines).

  Socrates prefaces the etymological inquiry by disavowing any competence to
  investigate the gods themselves (401a: "we do not think ourselves capable of
  investigating"), then introduces the name-givers with a remark that they
  were meteorologists and babblers (401b), combining epistemic modesty with a
  deprecating characterization of those whose work he is about to examine at
  length.
textual_basis: |
  At 401a Socrates says they will not investigate the gods because they do not
  consider themselves capable (ouk axioumen hoioi t' an einai skopein). At
  401b, immediately after proposing Hestia as the starting point, he describes
  the original name-givers as meteorologoi kai adoleschai tines.

  characterization; it does not claim that Socrates' subsequent etymologies
  are insincere or that the characterization is accurate.

greek_terms: [Ῥέαν, Κρόνον, ῥευμάτων, Ὠκεανόν, Τηθύν]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_2309
feature_label: mythic_authority_cited_by_name
observation: |
  Socrates invokes Heraclitus, Homer, Hesiod, and Orpheus as convergent authorities
  within the span 402b-402c to support the claim that the names Rhea, Cronus,
  Oceanus, and Tethys were given with an understanding of flux (rheumata).
  The span strings together poetic and philosophical authorities without
  distinguishing their epistemic status.
textual_basis: |
  At 402b Socrates asks whether the name-giver thought differently from
  Heraclitus when assigning Rhea and Cronus, both of which he connects to
  rheumata (flux-names). He then cites Homer for the phrase "Oceanus, genesis of
  the gods, and mother Tethys" (402b), adds "and I think Hesiod too" without a
  quotation, and then quotes Orpheus at length (402b-402c) on Oceanus and
  Tethys's marriage.
limits: |
  Does not establish whether the convergent reading is Socrates's own
  interpretation or a report of the name-giver's intention. The observation
  records the concatenation of authorities; it does not assess the accuracy of
  the Heraclitean reading.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_id: feature_candidate_2310
feature_label: socrates_invites_hermogenes_to_examine
observation: |
  After citing Heraclitus, Homer, Hesiod, and Orpheus in close succession,
  Socrates tells Hermogenes at 402c to "consider that these things both agree
  with one another and all point toward the doctrines of Heraclitus" (taut' oun
  skopei hoti kai allelois symphōnei kai pros ta tou Herakleitou panta teinei).
  The invitation to examine (skopei) is directed at Hermogenes rather than
  resolved by Socrates himself.
textual_basis: |
  At 402c Socrates uses the imperative skopei to hand the interpretive burden
  to Hermogenes. The claim that the poetic passages and Heraclitus are in
  harmony is presented as something for Hermogenes to examine, not as a settled
  conclusion demonstrated by Socrates. This follows a pattern earlier in the
  dialogue where Socrates defers or invites examination after making striking
  claims.
limits: |
  Does not determine whether Socrates believes the convergence or is testing
  Hermogenes. Does not assess the actual compatibility of Homer, Hesiod,
  Orpheus, and Heraclitus. The observation records only the deferral structure.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_family: definition_ladder

feature_label: etymology_as_definitional_analysis
observation: "Socrates offers an etymological analysis of the word anthropos (399c), claiming that it derives from the phrase 'anathrei ha opope' (he looks up at what he has seen). This functions as a definitional claim because the etymology asserts what distinguishes humans from other animals: humans alone reflect on what they see."
textual_basis: "At 399c, Socrates states that the name anthropos signifies that while other beasts do not examine or reckon what they see, the human alone both sees and then examines and reasons about what he has seen. The name is explicitly traced to anathron ha opope, with the alpha and accent changes explained at 399b as the sort of letter-removal and accent-shift that occur when a verb becomes a noun."
limits: "This observation records the etymological method as applied to a single term. It does not claim that Socrates endorses the etymology, nor does it assess whether the derivation is linguistically plausible."
review_status: accepted
```

limits: "This records a general method claim about phonetic transformation, not a theory of etymology. It does not assess whether the method is applied consistently across the dialogue."

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0109
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 401c-401d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt
  stephanus_span: 401c-401d
  start_marker: 401c
  end_marker: 401d
  start_char: 35231
  end_char: 36168
  text_sha256: 9c8d1c66229d984a0bd171a14e17d8c6b3fcf4e629d9acf56677a3b4a46dae39
greek_terms:
  - οὐσίαν
  - ἐσσίαν
  - ὠσίαν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: etymological_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_2345
feature_label: multiple_etymologies_single_name
observation: >
  Socrates subjects the word for being (ousia) to two competing etymological analyses through its dialectal variant forms. The variant "essia" yields a derivation connecting being to the goddess Hestia and to stability, while the variant "osia" yields a derivation connecting being to Heraclitean flux, traced to the verb "othoun" (pushing).
textual_basis: >
  At 401c-401d Socrates notes that what "we" call ousia, some call essia and others osia. From essia he derives the name Hestia and connects it to sacrificial practice honoring stability of being. From osia he derives a connection to the Heraclitean doctrine that all things move and nothing stays, with "othoun" as the causal principle, so that being is appropriately named osia.
limits: >
  This observation does not adjudicate which etymology Socrates prefers or whether he endorses the Heraclitean view. The passage presents the etymologies as analyses of what the name intends (bouletai), not as Socrates' own ontological commitments.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: god_name_equated_with_abstractum
observation: >
  The goddess Hestia is etymologically equated with the abstract concept of being (ousia/essia). Socrates argues that the being of things (he ton pragmaton ousia) is reasonably called Hestia, and that the cult practice of offering to Hestia first among all gods confirms that the name-givers understood Hestia to represent the being of all things.
textual_basis: >
  At 401c-401d Socrates states that according to the name essia, the being of things "has reason" (echei logon) to be called Hestia. He adds that those who named being essia appropriately offer first to Hestia before all gods (pro panton theon tei Hestiai protei prothuein), recognizing her as the being (ousia) of all things.
limits: >
  This observation records the etymological equation of a deity with an abstractum within the dialogue's theory of names. It does not assess whether the cult practice is accurately reported or whether Socrates endorses the theology implied.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0111
source_work: Cratylus

greek_terms:
  - Ἡράκλειτον
  - ὠσίαν
  - ὠθοῦν
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: etymological_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_2369
feature_label: etymology_supported_by_philosophical_doctrine
observation: >
  Socrates supports one etymology of ousia (the variant osia) by appeal to the philosophical doctrine of Heraclitus. He states that those who say "osia" would hold, in accordance with Heraclitus, that all beings move and nothing stays, with the cause and originating principle being "to othoun" (that which pushes), making "osia" a well-given name.
textual_basis: >
  At 401d Socrates explicitly invokes Heraclitus by name: those who use "osia" would hold "in accordance with Heraclitus" (kath' Herakleiton) that all beings go (ienai) and nothing stays (menein ouden). The etymology derives osia from othoun (pushing/thrusting), identified as the cause (to aition) and originating principle (to archegon) of all beings.
limits: >
  This observation records that a philosophical doctrine is cited as support for an etymological analysis. It does not evaluate whether the etymology is correct, whether Heraclitus would have endorsed it, or whether Socrates accepts the Heraclitean ontology.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_family: etymological_decomposition
feature_id: feature_candidate_2385
feature_label: divine_name_etymologized
observation: "Socrates etymologizes the divine name Poseidon by decomposing it into a descriptive phrase: the god who rules the sea is called Poseidon as though he were foot-bound, because the nature of the sea stopped him from walking and became a bond for his feet."
textual_basis: "At 402d Socrates announces the move to Zeus's brothers, then at 402e explains Poseidon's name: the first name-giver called him Poseidon because the sea stopped him from proceeding and became like a bond of the feet, hence the derivation to foot-bound."
limits: "This observation records the etymological method applied to a divine name; it does not evaluate the correctness of the etymology or claim anything about Socrates's belief in it."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "At 405c-405d Socrates offers four successive etymologies for the name of Apollo (Apollon): from 'washing away' (apolouon), from the Thessalian 'Aploun' (simple/true), from 'ever-shooting' (aeiballon), and from 'moving together' (homopolon) via the alpha prefix substitution."
textual_basis: "Within 405c-405d, Socrates explicitly strings together four distinct etymological accounts of the same divine name: apolouon (in his medical capacity), Aploun (mantic/truth role, with Thessalian attestation), aeiballon (archery), and the homopolon derivation through the alpha-alpha substitution argument involving music and astronomy at 405c-d."
limits: "This observation records the multiple-etymology structure for a single name. It does not assess whether the etymologies are philologically sound, whether Socrates endorses any of them, or whether the passage implies a unified theory of Apollo. It does not claim hidden intent or esoteric meaning."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0118
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 405c-405d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt
  stephanus_span: 405c-405d
  start_marker: 405c
  end_marker: 405d
  start_char: 43208
  end_char: 44192
  text_sha256: ccf3c2e6e64adbdffb0b179174d623eabeb0fe6cb1db31788ee99516db926b14
greek_terms: []
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2497
feature_label: etymology_uses_letter_substitution
observation: "At 405c-d Socrates explains Apollo's name by substituting the prefix alpha for homou ('together'): just as akolouthos and akoitin were derived from homokeleuthon and homokoitin through alpha substitution, so Apollo derives from homopolon."
textual_basis: "Socrates at 405c-d makes the substitution rule explicit: 'just as we called the homokeleuthon and homokoitin akolouthos and akoitin, replacing the homo- with a-, so too we called Apollona' (hosper oun ton homokeleuthon kai homokoitin akolouthon kai akoitin ekalesamen, metabalontes anti tou homo- a-, houto kai Apollona ekalesamen). He uses this to derive Apollo from homopolon, which itself rests on the claim that the alpha prefix often signifies 'together' (to alpha semainei pollachou to homou)."
limits: "This observation records the letter-substitution technique used within one etymology. It does not evaluate whether the alpha-homou equivalence is linguistically justified, how consistently the technique is used across the Cratylus, or whether Socrates presents it as serious or playful. Does not claim esotericism."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0138

  text_sha256: c3fa1ce5427ffcfbebaff855c8a1cb978c46293ff03f3522859179a2ea6ebbca
greek_terms:
  - πῦρ
  - ὕδωρ
  - κύνας
  - ἀὴρ
  - ἀήτας
  - ἀητόρρουν
  - ἀειθεὴρ
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: etymological_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_2624
feature_label: etymology_from_compound
observation: |
  Socrates proposes multiple etymological derivations for the name of air (aer): (i) it is called aer because it raises things from the earth, (ii) because it always flows, or (iii) because poets call wind-streams aetas, from which one might coin the compound aetorroun (wind-flowing).
textual_basis: |
  At 410b Socrates explicitly lists three alternative etymological hypotheses for aer in rapid succession, citing the verb for raising, the phrase "always flows," and poetic usage of aetas. He then coins the compound aetorroun as a plausible form derived from the poetic term.
limits: |
  The passage does not settle on one etymology; it presents competing possibilities without committing to any. The observation records the form-level etymological method (compound and phonetic variation) without assessing whether the proposed derivations are linguistically accurate.
review_status: accepted
```

  - κύνας

feature_id: feature_candidate_2625
feature_label: dialectal_variant_aids_etymology
observation: |
  Socrates appeals to a foreign linguistic variant to explain the difficulty of assigning a Greek etymology to fire (pur): the Phrygians call it by a closely similar name with a slight deviation, suggesting that the word may be barbarian rather than native Greek. He extends the same pattern to water and dogs as other words possibly not native Greek.
textual_basis: |
  At 410a Socrates explicitly raises the possibility that pur may be a barbarian word, then notes that the Phrygians call it with a slight inflection. He immediately extends the pattern to hudor and kunas as other words that may not be natively Greek.
limits: |
  The observation registers Socrates' use of a dialectal or foreign-language parallel as an etymological resource, not a substantive claim that pur is in fact barbarian. The passage leaves open whether the word can be explained in Greek.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: |
  Socrates describes the customary name Selanaia as a compressed (sygkekrotemenon) form of a fuller expression Selaenoneoaeia. The etymology works by positing an underlying descriptive phrase that is then concealed through phonetic compression in ordinary usage.
textual_basis: |
  At 409b-c Socrates constructs Selaenoneoaeia from selas (light), neon (new), and henon (old), explaining the moon's name. He then states that this is compressed (sygkekrotemenon) into Selanaia, the name many use (409b).
limits: |
  The observation does not assess the etymological validity. It only records the compression-from-full-expression pattern as a recurring device in the Cratylus.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: name_givers_vertigo_as_flux_cause
observation: "Socrates claims that the ancient name-givers, like many contemporary wise men, suffered from vertigo (eiliogiosin) from turning around too much in their search for how things are, and that this caused them to believe that all things are in motion and flux (411b-411c)."
textual_basis: "At 411b Socrates says the very ancient name-givers 'like many of the wise among the present day, from turning around frequently while investigating how beings are, get dizzy, and then it appears to them that things revolve and are altogether carried along' (411b). He connects this vertigo-induced belief directly to the name-giving practice."
limits: "This observation records the etiology Socrates gives for the flux-belief underlying the names; it does not assess whether Socrates endorses or ironizes this diagnosis, nor does it evaluate the historical accuracy of the claim."
review_status: accepted
```

  pragmasi to synienai (for synienai means the soul going together with
  things).

feature_id: feature_candidate_2763
feature_label: etymology_supported_by_poetic_and_prosopographic_evidence
observation: >
  Socrates derives sophia (wisdom) from contact with motion (phoras
  ephaptesthai), and supports the etymology with two external citations: poetic
  usage of esythe (rushed) for rapid motion, and the Spartan proper name Sous
  (from soo, Doric for rapid impulse) as used by the Lacedaemonians (412b).
textual_basis: >
  At 412b Socrates says sophia means contact with motion, calls the derivation
  darker and more foreign, then appeals to poets' use of esythe and the
  Laconian name Sous to support the connection between sophia and rapid motion.
limits: >
  This records the appeal to external evidence (poetry, onomastics) within
  the etymological procedure, without endorsing or rejecting the derivation.
review_status: accepted
```

textual_basis: "At 415d, Socrates says the correct name would be aeireiten (ever-flowing) and explains the contraction to arete, then notes Hermogenes may again accuse him of fabricating (plassein). He qualifies the etymology by saying it holds if his earlier account was correct."
limits: "This observation does not assess whether the etymology is linguistically sound or whether Socrates is endorsing it seriously; it only records the etymological procedure and its stated conditions."

textual_basis: "Socrates says: ego de, o Hermogenes, hate lipares on peri autou, tauta men panta diapepusmai en aporrhetois, hoti touto esti to dikaion kai to aition — I, since I am persistent about it, have inquired into all these things in secret (412e-413a). He attributes the identification of the just/cause with Zeus (Dia) to an unnamed someone (ephe tis, 'someone said')."
limits: "This observation records the transmission frame Socrates uses (secret inquiry from unnamed sources). It does not assess whether such teachings existed historically or whether Socrates is being ironic."

  Socrates explains that original names have been buried (katakechōstai) by those who
  wish to make them tragic, adding and removing letters for euphony and adornment,
  and by the passage of time.
textual_basis: >
  At 414c, Socrates states that the first names (ta prōta onomata tethenta) have
  been covered over by those who want to "tragedize" them (tragōidein), who add and
  remove letters for the sake of euphony (eustomias heneka), turning them in every
  direction, and also by adornment (kallōpismou) and time (chronou).
limits: >
  This observation records the stated mechanism of phonetic alteration; it does not
  evaluate whether the mechanism is historically accurate or whether Socrates
  endorses it as the correct account.
review_status: accepted

textual_basis: At 417e Hermogenes says 'variegated, Socrates, are the names that come out for you. For now you seemed to me, just like the prelude of the nomos of Athena, to have played the aulos' (stomaulesai), framing the preceding etymology as a musical prelude.
limits: This records Hermogenes' characterization of Socrates' speech at the close of 417e; it does not interpret whether this is praise or gentle mockery.

english_gloss: ""

feature_id: feature_candidate_2954
feature_label: etymology_by_phonetic_gloss
observation: >
  Socrates traces the name eros through an archaic form esros, explaining the present form
  eros as the result of a vowel substitution: the letter omega replaced the earlier ou
  diphthong. The derivation operates on phonetic change alone, treating the shift from
  ou to o as the complete explanation for the current form of the word.
textual_basis: >
  At 420b Socrates states that eros was formerly called esros because of the old use of
  ou instead of omega (to gar ou anti tou o echrometha), and that the present name eros
  results from the substitution of omega for ou (dia ten tou o anti tou ou metallagen).
  The derivation is purely phonetic, with no semantic argument beyond the inflow imagery
  already given.
limits: >
  This observation notes the phonetic-etymology pattern for eros. It does not claim that the
  derivation is linguistically accurate or that the Cratylus consistently prefers phonetic
  over semantic derivations.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: >
  Socrates etymologizes onoma (name) by decomposing it into a compound expression:
  on (being) + hou masma estin (of which there is a search). He further notes that
  the derivation is even clearer in the related form onomaston (nameable), which he
  glosses as "being of which there is a search" (421a).
textual_basis: >
  At 421a Socrates states that onoma appears to be compounded from a phrase
  (ek logou onomati synkekrotemeno) meaning "this is being (on) of which there
  happens to be a search (zetema)." He then points to onomaston as making the
  derivation explicit: touto einai on hou masma estin.
limits: >
  This observation records only the etymological decomposition as presented;
  it does not assess phonetic plausibility or whether Plato endorses the etymology.
review_status: accepted
```

  calling them "the greatest and finest" (ta megista kai ta kallista, 421a).

  At 421a Hermogenes announces "I ask then about the greatest and the finest —
  truth and falsehood and being and this very thing about which our discussion now is,
  name, why name has its name." This explicitly sets the agenda for the next
  phase of etymological inquiry.

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0181

greek_terms: ["στοιχεῖα", "ἀγαθὸν", "ἀγαστοῦ", "θοοῦ"]
english_gloss: "Socrates posits that some names are elemental (stoicheia) and not further reducible, using agathon as a name already decomposed."
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2956
feature_label: definition_by_etymology
observation: "Socrates proposes that the inquiry into names must eventually reach elemental names (stoicheia) that are not composed of further names, and if a name is so composed its elements must themselves be investigated, with the result that the respondent will be compelled to give up unless such primary names are reached."
textual_basis: "At 421e-422a Socrates says the questioner will keep asking about the words (rhemata) by which a name is explained, and then about those words in turn, forcing the answerer to give up (apeipein) unless he arrives at names that are like elements (stoicheia) of all other words and names (422a). The example agathon, previously analyzed from agaston and thoon (422a), illustrates a name that is not elemental and therefore requires further decomposition."
limits: "This observation records the methodological claim about elemental names and the infinite regress threat; it does not evaluate whether the proposed etymology for agathon is correct or whether stoicheia actually function as claimed."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: definition_target_set
observation: |
  Socrates announces that the inquiry has reached the level of primary names (stoicheia), and that the correctness of these primary names must be investigated by a different method than the one used for composite names.
textual_basis: |
  At 422b Socrates states that if they arrive at a name no longer composed of other names, they would rightly say they are at the element (stoicheion) and no longer need to refer it to other names. At 422b-c he asks Hermogenes whether the names now in question are such elements and whether their correctness must be examined in some other manner. At 422c he invites Hermogenes to join him in examining what the correctness of primary names must be like.
limits: |
  This records the procedural marking of a new definitional target — primary names — but does not assert what the new method is or whether it succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: definition_chain_consolidated
observation: "Socrates consolidates the preceding etymological survey by asserting that all the names they have reviewed aim to reveal what each being is (422d). He then distinguishes between two classes of name: secondary (hustera) names and primary (prota) names, and claims that secondary names accomplish their revealing function through the prior (protera) ones (422d)."
textual_basis: "Socrates says the correctness of the names they have covered 'wanted to be such as to make clear what each of the beings is' (422d). Immediately after Hermogenes agrees, Socrates says 'the primary names must have this [capacity to reveal beings] no less than the secondary ones,' and adds that 'the secondary names, it seems, were able to accomplish this through the prior ones' (422d)."
limits: "The observation records the distinction and dependency claim. It does not assess whether the etymological survey actually succeeded in revealing beings, nor does it evaluate the coherence of the primary-secondary distinction."
review_status: accepted
```

english_gloss: "if we had no voice or tongue; with hands and head and rest of body; the mute"
feature_family: hypothetical_prompt_or_scenario
feature_id: feature_candidate_2969
feature_label: hypothetical_prompt_or_scenario
observation: "To motivate the problem of how primary names can reveal beings, Socrates introduces a hypothetical scenario: if we had no voice and no tongue but still wanted to indicate things to one another, would we not, like those who are mute (hoi eneoi), try to signify with hands, head, and the rest of the body (422e)?"
textual_basis: "Socrates asks Hermogenes: 'if we had no voice nor tongue, but wanted to make things clear to each other, wouldn't we, just as the mute do now, attempt to signify with hands and head and the rest of the body?' Hermogenes agrees there is no other way (422e)."
limits: "The observation notes the hypothetical scenario as a turn device. It does not claim that the scenario resolves the problem of primary names, nor does it evaluate the adequacy of gestural signification."
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: definition_proposed
observation: "The segment repeatedly characterizes the function of names with verbs of revealing and signifying: names aim to make clear (deloun) what each being is (422d), primary names must make beings manifest (phanera) if they are to be names (422d-422e), and the hypothetical gesture-language would signify (semainein) things (422e)."
textual_basis: "At 422d Socrates says the correctness of names they reviewed 'wanted to be such as to make clear (deloun) what each being is.' At 422d-422e he asks how primary names 'will make beings manifest (phanera) to us.' At 422e he asks whether without voice we would 'signify (semainein) with hands and head.' Hermogenes assents across all three formulations."
limits: "The observation identifies the recurrence of a revealing-and-signifying function vocabulary. It does not evaluate whether names actually perform this function or whether the vocabulary is applied consistently across primary and secondary names."
review_status: accepted
```

  - δηλοῦν

english_gloss: "imitation, making clear, signifying through bodily mimesis"
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1293
feature_label: definition_supported_by_example
observation: "Socrates grounds the definition of name as vocal imitation by first describing a pre-linguistic bodily mimesis at 423a: raising a hand toward the sky to signify the upward and light, toward the earth for the downward and heavy, and shaping one's body and posture like a running horse or other animal. These examples establish that signification occurs through imitation of the nature of the thing before the argument shifts to vocal imitation."
textual_basis: "At 423a Socrates says: if we wished to make clear the upward and light, we would raise our hand toward the sky, imitating the very nature of the thing; if the downward and heavy, toward the earth. And if we wished to make clear a running horse or any other animal, you know that we would make our own bodies and postures as like them as possible. These bodily examples are explicitly called deloma at 423a-b and provide the analogical basis for the vocal definition that follows."
limits: "This observation records the analogical scaffolding. It does not claim that the bodily examples prove the vocal definition correct, only that Socrates uses them to introduce the mimetic account of naming."
review_status: accepted
```

observation: "Socrates derives kerdos (gain) from kerannutai (it is mixed) by a letter substitution: the name-giver originally used nu (n) but inserted delta (d) instead, producing kerdos."
textual_basis: "At 417b, Socrates says the name kerdos 'makes clear what it intends' when one gives back nu instead of delta, because the good 'is mixed into everything as it goes through.' He states the name-giver inserted delta instead of nu when uttering kerdos."
limits: "This observation records only the letter-substitution mechanism Socrates claims. It does not evaluate the linguistic accuracy of the etymology or whether kerdos is historically derived from kerannutai."
review_status: accepted

textual_basis: "At 417b-417c, Socrates rejects the ordinary meaning of lysiteloun as repaying expenditure (used by kapeloi, retail traders) and instead derives it from dissolving the end of motion: because it is the swiftest thing of what is, not allowing things to stand still or reach an end, but always dissolving any end that tries to arise in motion, making it unceasing and deathless. He concludes the good was called lysiteloun as 'that which dissolves the end of motion.'"
limits: "This observation records Socrates' derivation of the term from motion vocabulary. It does not evaluate linguistic validity or address whether this etymology coheres with other Socratic claims about the good."
review_status: accepted

textual_basis: At 423c Socrates says the account of names as imitating sheep, cocks, and other animals is not well stated, and Hermogenes concedes. Hermogenes then asks at 423c what kind of mimesis a name would be (tis an mimesis eie to onoma), which reframes the definiendum from simple vocal imitation to a qualified mimesis.
limits: The record does not assert that a satisfactory definition is reached in this span, only that the candidate account is rejected and the definiendum is revised.

observation: Socrates distinguishes the naming craft (onomastike techne) from music and drawing by arguing that imitating the sound and shape of things belongs to music and drawing, not to naming.
textual_basis: At 423c-d Socrates states that things have voice (phone) and shape (schema) and color (chroma), and that imitating these belongs to music and drawing respectively. He concludes that the naming craft is not about these kinds of imitations (423d).
limits: The record only notes the distinction between naming and neighboring crafts; it does not claim that a positive account of the naming craft is given in this span.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Hermogenes immediately concedes two Socratic objections in succession: first that miming animals is not naming (423c), then that such an account is not satisfactory (423c).
textual_basis: After Socrates objects at 423c, Hermogenes replies alethe legeis (you speak truly). When Socrates then asks whether the account seems satisfactory, Hermogenes replies ouk emoige (not to me). Both are unqualified concessions.
limits: The record does not claim the concessions are coerced or that Hermogenes fully understands the implications; it only notes the rapid assent pattern.
review_status: accepted
```

  At 424d Socrates asks whether the realities (ta onta) into which names are to be placed contain eide in the same manner as the phonetic stoicheia contain eide.
textual_basis: |
  At 424d the text poses the question whether there are eide in the onta in the same way as in the stoicheia (letters). This maps the concept of kinds from the domain of phonetic elements onto the domain of named beings.
limits: |
  The passage does not develop a theory of forms or participation; it only poses an analogical question about whether the same structure of kinds-within-elements applies to both letters and named realities.

```

  From 424d to 424e Socrates develops the comparison: painters aiming at likeness sometimes apply only ostreon, sometimes mix many pigments, as when they produce a human figure (andreikelon). He then maps this onto combining stoicheia to form syllables and names.

observation: "Socrates proposes that the true name-maker is the person who can imitate the essence of each thing through letters and syllables, and Hermogenes endorses this as the definition of what they have been seeking since earlier in the dialogue."
textual_basis: "At 423e-424a Socrates asks: 'if someone could imitate this very thing, the essence of each, through letters and syllables, wouldn't he reveal what each thing is?' Hermogenes assents and identifies this figure as the name-maker, saying 'this seems to me, Socrates, to be exactly what we have been seeking for a long time.' This is a direct definitional proposal building on the earlier discussion of the name-maker's art."
limits: "This observation notes the proposal itself and its acceptance by Hermogenes, not whether the definition succeeds or survives later testing. It does not claim that Socrates endorses this as his own final view."
review_status: accepted
```

  - βολῆς
  - ἀβουλία
feature_family: etymology_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_2897
feature_label: etymology_from_compound
observation: Socrates etymologizes a series of terms (oiesis, boule, boulesthai, bouleuesthai) by deriving them from a shared stem related to shooting or throwing (bole). He also derives aboulia by privative contrast.
textual_basis: At 420c, Socrates says oiesis harmonizes with this, then connects oiesis explicitly to oisis (the movement of the soul toward every thing) and to bole (a throw). He links boule, boulesthai, bouleuesthai as likenesses following upon bole. Aboulia is then derived as atuchia—missing the mark (ou balontos oude tuchontos).
limits: This observation notes the etymological compounding method; it does not evaluate the correctness of the derivations or claim any hidden doctrine.
review_status: accepted
```

greek_terms:
  - ἑκούσιον
  - εἶκον
  - ἀναγκαῖον
  - ἀντίτυπον
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_2956
feature_label: definition_by_etymology
observation: Socrates provides an etymological definition of hekousion (voluntary) as that which yields (eikon) to the going (ionti) and does not resist, proceeding according to wish (boulesin). Anankaion (compulsory) is defined by contrast as that which resists and goes against wish.
textual_basis: At 420c-d, Socrates moves to hekousion and explicitly defines it: to eikon kai me antitupoun all' eikon to ionti, generated kata ten boulesin. Anankaion and antitupon are defined para ten boulesin, linked to error and ignorance.
limits: This observation notes the etymological definition technique; it does not assess whether the distinction between voluntary and compulsory is adequate in philosophical terms.
review_status: accepted
```

feature_label: respondent_procedural_complaint
observation: Hermogenes interrupts Socrates's rapid etymological chain with a procedural remark that Socrates is bringing these derivations more densely (puknotera) together.
textual_basis: At 420c-d, after the series of oiesis/boule/aboulia derivations, Hermogenes says: tauta ede moi dokeis, o Sokrates, puknotera epagein. Socrates acknowledges the remark and states he is running toward the finish (telos gar ede theo).
limits: This observation records the pace-of-argument comment; it does not interpret the remark as irony, resistance, or doctrinal challenge.
review_status: accepted
```

  Socrates states a general principle of name-analysis: adding or removing letters and slightly twisting them can so alter the meaning of names that they come to signify the opposite.
textual_basis: |
  At 418a Socrates declares that those who add and remove letters greatly alter the meanings of names, sometimes reversing the signification through minor distortions.
limits: |

greek_terms:
  - πρῶτα ὀνόματα
  - ὕστερα
english_gloss: primary names, secondary ones
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1573
feature_label: deficiendum_marked_for_inquiry
observation: Socrates asserts an epistemic dependency: one who does not know the correctness of primary names cannot know the correctness of secondary names, since secondary names must be clarified from the primary ones about which one knows nothing.
textual_basis: At 426a-b Socrates states that without knowledge of primary names (protôn onomatôn) one cannot know the secondary names (husterôn) which are necessarily clarified (dêlousthai) from the primary ones.
limits: Socrates does not yet define what qualifies as a primary versus secondary name or how the correctness relation is transmitted; the observation captures only the dependency claim.
review_status: accepted
```

observation: Socrates recalls a previously established methodological resource for etymological inquiry: when the origin of a name is unknown, it can be called barbarian (foreign).
textual_basis: At 421c-d, Socrates says they have already provided themselves with one resource so as to seem to say something when answering — namely, to claim that whatever they do not know is something barbarian.
limits: This observation registers a methodological default in etymological inquiry, not a claim about actual linguistic borrowing from non-Greek languages.

feature_label: antiquity_obscures_primary_names
observation: Socrates proposes that the earliest (primary) names may be undiscoverable due to their antiquity and because names have been twisted in every direction, making ancient speech indistinguishable from foreign speech.
textual_basis: At 421d, Socrates says it may be that, due to antiquity, the first names are undiscoverable; for because names are twisted in every way, it is no wonder if the ancient speech differs in no way from barbarian speech.
limits: This records a stated limitation on etymological method; it does not claim that Socrates endorses this as the final explanation, nor does it assert that primary names are in fact undiscoverable.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  text_sha256: 52a88565c5a0795e4747fad775c41d9b41e5fa3b1209e60fccebbb3b9bef6637
greek_terms: ["ῥῶ", "κίνησις", "ῥεῖν", "ῥοῇ"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: etymology_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_2159
feature_label: name_etymology_via_concealment
observation: Socrates explains that kinesis conceals an older form hesis, revealing that the name has undergone phonetic modification that obscures its original motivation. He traces the modern form kinesis to three factors: a foreign word kiein, a vowel change from ei to eta, and insertion of nu. He says it ought to be kieinesis if formed correctly.
textual_basis: At 426c Socrates says kinesis derives from hesis (with archaic ei used for eta), rooted in the foreign kiein meaning ienai. He then states that through the insertion of nu and the vowel change it became kinesis when it should be kieinesis.
limits: This observation records only the etymological method deployed in this span. It does not claim that the etymology is historically accurate or that the name-giver intended this concealment.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms: ["ῥῶ", "κίνησις", "φορά", "ῥεῖν", "ῥοῇ"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_3058
feature_label: letter_as_phonetic_instrument
observation: Socrates treats the letter rho as an instrument (organon) of motion, claiming that the name-giver employed it as a tool for imitating (aphomoioun) phora (locomotion). He supports this by citing multiple words containing rho that involve flowing or motion: rhein and rhoe at 426d.
textual_basis: At 426c Socrates calls rho a kalon organon of kinesis, used by the name-giver pros to aphomoioun tei phorai. At 426d he gives examples of rhein and rhoe where the letter rho imitates phora.
limits: This observation records the phonetic-imitation claim within this segment only. It does not evaluate whether the analysis of rho is consistent across the dialogue or whether it is linguistically defensible.
review_status: accepted
```
