```yaml
feature_label: etymology_from_compound
observation: Socrates derives stasis from apophasis of ienai (negation of going), explaining that the name was modified for the sake of ornament (kallopismon). This etymology treats the word as a compound whose surface form conceals an underlying privative sense.
textual_basis: At 426d Socrates states that stasis boulesthai einai apophasis tou ienai (stasis wants to be a negation of going) but was named stasis dia de ton kallopismon (through ornamentation).
limits: This observation records only the etymological claim as presented. It does not assert that stasis is historically a compound or that the kallopismon explanation is the only one offered.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
limits: Does not establish that Socrates himself considers the method ridiculous. The observation records a self-deprecating forecast about the reception of the method, which may function as a rhetorical hedge.
```

```yaml
  Socrates opens his reply to Cratylus by declaring amazement at his own
  wisdom and distrust of it, then articulates a principle that self-deception
  is the hardest form of deception because the deceiver is always present
  (428d).
textual_basis: >-
  Socrates says: "Good Cratylus, I too have long been amazed at my own wisdom
  and distrust it." He then states that being deceived by oneself is the
  most difficult of all things, because "the deceiver does not depart even a
  little but is always present" (428d). This is an explicit knowledge-
  disavowal coupled with a general principle about the danger of self-
  deception.
limits: >-
  Does not determine whether Socrates is sincere, ironic, or performing. Only
  records the explicit textual claim of self-distrust and the
  characterization of self-deception.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates restates the working definition of name-correctness — that it indicates what the thing is — and asks Cratylus to confirm that the formulation has been adequately stated, before advancing to the next argumentative step.
textual_basis: At 428e Socrates says that the correctness of a name is that which will indicate what the thing is, then asks whether this has been adequately stated. Cratylus responds with unusually strong assent, which closes the definitional phase and allows Socrates to pivot to the teaching function of names.
limits: This observation only notes the procedural restatement and confirmation; it does not evaluate whether the definition is correct or whether Cratylus fully understands it.
```

```yaml

greek_terms: ["διανομήν", "ὀρθήν", "ἀληθῆ", "ψευδῆ"]
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_1832
feature_label: definition_by_dichotomous_contrast
observation: Socrates defines correct distribution (orthē dianomē) through the criterion of assigning what is fitting and similar to each recipient, then extends the definition to names by adding truth as an additional condition, contrasting it with the incorrect distribution of the dissimilar which is false when applied to names.
textual_basis: At 430c, Socrates secures Cratylus's assent that correct distribution assigns to each what is fitting and similar (to prosēkon te kai to homoion). At 430d, he defines the correct distribution as orthē for both imitations, adding alēthē as a further requirement specific to names, and labels the opposite distribution as ouk orthēn and pseudē when it concerns names.
limits: This is Socrates's proposed working terminology for the argument, not an independently established definition; Cratylus has not yet fully accepted it.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: name_as_mimetic_image
observation: Socrates characterizes a name as an image produced by imitating the being of things through syllables and letters, with quality depending on whether all fitting elements are rendered.
textual_basis: At 431d Socrates describes the person who imitates the being of things through syllables and letters as producing an image, and states that this image is a name. The image is fine if all fitting elements are rendered, and an image but not fine if anything is omitted or added.
limits: The passage assumes rather than argues for the mimetic theory of names; it does not explain how syllables and letters imitate the being of things.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_925
feature_label: hypothesis_for_examination
observation: Cratylus states the thesis that false speech is impossible, grounding it in the claim that whenever someone says something, they necessarily say "what is" (to on), and that speaking falsely just is "saying things that are not" (mē ta onta legein).
textual_basis: At 429d Cratylus responds to Socrates' question by asking how anyone speaking could fail to say what is (to on), and defines false speech as saying things that are not (mē ta onta legein). Socrates then immediately begins testing this thesis.
limits: This observation records the thesis and its definitional grounding in the passage. It does not assess whether Cratylus's position is coherent or whether Socrates' subsequent refutation succeeds.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

english_gloss: ""

feature_label: terminological_control
observation: Socrates probes the scope of Cratylus's thesis by distinguishing four related speech-act verbs — legein (to speak), phanai (to assert), eipein (to say), and proseipein (to address) — and asking whether Cratylus maintains that falsehood is impossible for each distinct verb.
textual_basis: At 429d-e Socrates first asks whether Cratylus distinguishes between legein and phanai (429d-e), and after Cratylus rejects both, extends the question to eipein and proseipein (429e), enumerating all four verbs in a single question to test whether Cratylus's thesis covers each term.
limits: This observation records Socrates' terminological probing strategy. It does not claim that the four verbs carry distinct semantic weight in Cratylus's eventual response.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0237

greek_terms:
  - ἄλλως ἂν οὗτος ταῦτα φθέγξασθαι
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_2885
feature_label: hypothetical_prompt_or_scenario
observation: Socrates constructs a concrete hypothetical counterexample to test Cratylus's thesis that false speech is impossible: someone meeting Cratylus abroad, taking his hand, and addressing him with the name of Hermogenes. Cratylus dismisses the counterexample by claiming the speaker would merely be producing sounds without meaning (allōs phthenxasthai), thereby preserving his thesis.
textual_basis: At 429e Socrates describes a scenario where a person greets Cratylus but addresses him as "Hermogenes, son of Smicrion." Cratylus responds that such a person would simply be uttering these words "without meaning" (allōs), avoiding the conclusion that false address has occurred.
limits: This observation records the structure of the counterexample and Cratylus's defensive response. It does not evaluate whether Cratylus's dismissal of the counterexample is philosophically adequate.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 429b Socrates secures Cratylus's assent that all names (at least all that are names) are correctly set. Cratylus concedes the name only seems to belong to Hermogenes but really belongs to another. At 429c Socrates pushes further, asking whether someone who calls him Hermogenes speaks falsely."
```

```yaml
  asserting that there is a correctness of names, but saying nothing clear
```

```yaml
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_1724
feature_label: respondent_challenges_procedure
observation: >
  Cratylus, when summoned to respond to the preceding account of names, pushes
  back by questioning whether it is plausible to learn and teach any matter
  quickly, let alone one that seems to be among the greatest matters.
textual_basis: >
  At 427e Cratylus responds to Hermogenes' demand with a counter-question:
  "Does it seem easy to you, Hermogenes, to learn and teach any matter so
  quickly, let alone one so great, which indeed seems to be among the greatest
  things?" He challenges the procedural assumption that he can produce an
  immediate judgment on the correctness of names.
limits: >
  This records Cratylus's procedural challenge, not whether his objection is
  valid or whether the preceding Socratic account is correct. The observation
  does not adjudicate the substantive dispute about names.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0244
source_work: Cratylus

  text_sha256: 1f96f716ac24d63185d286ae9cf8ec047fe891909fb065352c58d8d4a8da5d98
greek_terms: [μίμημά, μιμήματα, ζωγραφήματα]
english_gloss: "Socrates secures Cratylus's agreement that a name (onoma) is a kind of mimesis (imitation) of a thing (pragma) and that painted images (zographmata) are another kind of mimesis of things."
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_1299
feature_label: painting_as_speech_analogy
observation: "At 430a-430b Socrates gains Cratylus's assent to two parallel claims: a name is an imitation (mimema) of a thing, and painted images are also imitations of things, setting up an analogy between name-making and painting as two species of mimetic production."
textual_basis: "Socrates asks 'do you not also agree that the name is a kind of imitation of the thing?' (430a-b) and Cratylus assents 'most of all' (pantn malista). Socrates then asks 'do you not also say that paintings are in another way imitations of certain things?' and Cratylus says yes. This establishes a parallel between names and paintings as two kinds of mimemata."
limits: "The observation does not assess whether the painting-name analogy succeeds or whether Cratylus's naturalist theory of names depends on it. It records only the analogy's introduction."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_label: false_dichotomy_rejected
observation: "At 430a Cratylus rejects the very possibility of speaking partly true and partly false by comparing false speech to meaningless noise (psophein) — someone striking a bronze pot and moving himself in vain. This dismisses the mixed-position Socrates offered as a compromise."
textual_basis: "When Socrates asks whether a speaker could utter things that are partly true and partly false (430a), Cratylus responds that such a person would merely make noise (psophein), vainly moving himself like someone who struck and moved a bronze pot. This refuses Socrates's proposed middle ground."
limits: "The observation records Cratylus's rejection of the mixed-transmission claim and the noise analogy. It does not evaluate whether his collapse of false speech into meaningless noise is defensible."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "Socrates says at 430b: 'Is it possible to distribute and assign both these imitations, the paintings and those names, to the things of which they are imitations, or not?' He prefaces this with a hedge: 'perhaps I do not understand what you are saying' (iss gar eg ou manthan)."
limits: "The observation records the procedural framing. It does not track whether the distribution succeeds or whether Cratylus answers, which falls beyond 430b."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_405
feature_label: craft_analogy_as_counterargument
observation: >
  Socrates counters Cratylus's claim that any addition or subtraction to a name makes it no longer that name, by introducing a distinction between the correctness criterion for numbers (where any change produces a new number) and the correctness criterion for qualitative images (where perfect reproduction would destroy the image).
textual_basis: >
  At 432a-b, Socrates concedes Cratylus's principle for numbers (like the number ten), but challenges its application to images: he argues that a qualitative image (eikon) would cease to be an image if it reproduced every feature of its original exactly.
limits: >
  This observation records the analogy structure and its argumentative use; it does not evaluate the strength of the analogy or its later consequences.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0250

  text_sha256: 761974b045c0efdaed48ed34721a2fd93c4fcde0e63f17bedc038977f98bcc4d
greek_terms:
  - γράμματα
  - συλλαβαῖς
  - δήλωμα
  - εἰκών
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_3101
feature_label: name_as_mimetic_image
observation: At 433b-c, Socrates explains that a name correctly given must have appropriate letters that are like the thing, and that a name that is not well given may still be mostly composed of appropriate and like letters but contain something unfitting, just as an image can be mostly fitting but flawed.
textual_basis: At 433b Socrates proposes that a name is a disclosure of a thing by means of syllables and letters. He then asks whether a well-given name must have appropriate letters, which Cratylus affirms, and whether the appropriate ones are those like the things, which he also affirms. At 433c Socrates extends this by positing that a not-well-given name would still be mostly composed of fitting letters but contain something unfitting, which would prevent the name from being beautiful or well-wrought, explicitly using the image analogy.
limits: This observation records the mimetic conception of name as image, not whether Plato endorses it. It does not address the earlier or later arguments against this mimetic theory elsewhere in the dialogue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_1190
feature_label: mimesis_appearance_reality_distinction
observation: "Socrates argues that if a name were a perfect likeness of its named object in every respect, every name would produce two of everything and one could no longer distinguish which is the thing itself and which is the name."
textual_basis: "At 432c-d Socrates posits a thought-experiment: if someone reproduced all of Cratylus's attributes—heat, motion, soul, intelligence—in another, then there would be two Cratyluses, not one image. He then argues that images are not required to possess everything their originals have, which leads to the conclusion that names are images and a criterion of correctness different from total replication is needed."
limits: "This observation records the use of a copy/identity thought-experiment to delimit the correctness of images and names. It does not assert that Socrates endorses a specific doctrine of participation or that the passage is esoteric."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0252
source_work: Cratylus

  text_sha256: cc73d1fddf20fb2c949b666c57836dd59d21f61c31feccc85c7a38afcd827ae3
greek_terms:
  - στοιχεῖα
  - ζωγράφημα
  - φαρμακεῖα
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_3122
feature_label: painting_as_name_craft_analogy
observation: At 434a-b Socrates argues that if a name is to resemble its object, the elementary components (stoicheia) from which primary names are composed must themselves bear a natural resemblance to the things they name. He supports this by an explicit analogy with painting: just as a painter could not compose a likeness of any being unless the pigments were by nature similar to the things painting imitates, so names cannot become like anything unless their constitutive elements already possess some similarity to what the names imitate.
textual_basis: Socrates introduces the painting analogy at 434a ("unless the pigments were by nature similar…") and then maps it explicitly onto names at 434b. The analogy is structural: the relation of pigments to painted images parallels the relation of stoicheia to names.
limits: The observation records the structural use of a craft analogy but does not assess whether the analogy succeeds on its own terms. The passage does not specify which particular stoicheia are at issue.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_id: feature_candidate_3123
feature_label: primary_names_hypothesis
observation: At 434a Socrates refers to a class of "primary names" (ta prota onomata) that are constructed from elementary components (stoicheia). This passage resumes the earlier hypothesis that names are built up from elemental sound-units that imitate the nature of things, and it distinguishes primary names from derivative or complex names on the basis of their compositional level.
textual_basis: Socrates states at 434a that if a name is to be like the thing, the stoicheia out of which someone will compose the primary names must be like the things. The phrase ta prota onomata marks a distinct class of names.
limits: The observation records the re-introduction of the primary-names hypothesis in the argument; it does not evaluate whether the compositional account of naming is defensible or whether the primary-names class is coherent.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  At 434c Socrates explicitly states that Athenians say one form for hardness while Eretrians say another (ending in -ter instead of -tes). He then uses this variation to question whether rho and sigma both indicate the same thing (hardness) or only one does.
limits: |
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0261
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 433d-433e
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt
  stephanus_span: 433d-433e
  start_marker: "433d"
  end_marker: "433e"

  text_sha256: "5a9e6040b6cdfacb73799209398efa7ca66616ffdd9a34b563a9d6d887977df8"
greek_terms:
  - συνθήματα
  - συνθήκην
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_380
feature_label: forced_alternative
observation: >
  At 433d-e Socrates presents Cratylus with a forced choice between two mutually exclusive
  theories of how primary names become disclosures: the natural-likeness view (make the name
  as much like the thing disclosed as possible) and the convention view (names are agreements,
  their correctness is convention, and it makes no difference whether small is called large or
  vice versa). He asks which manner of explanation pleases Cratylus more.
textual_basis: >
  Socrates uses the disjunctive construction framing two incompatible accounts, explicitly
  naming Hermogenes as a representative of the convention view. The convention position is
  spelled out with the extreme example of calling small "large" and large "small" by agreement,
  marking it as a genuine alternative rather than a straw man.
limits: >
  The observation records only the structural presentation of a forced alternative; it does
  not determine which view Socrates or Plato endorses, nor does it assess the philosophical
  merits of either theory.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

greek_terms:
  - ζωγραφήμασιν
  - χρώματά
  - σχήματα
  - εἰκόνας
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: craft_analogy
feature_id: feature_candidate_1299
feature_label: painting_as_speech_analogy
observation: >
  Socrates develops the painting analogy for name-making by introducing
  gradations of correctness: just as in paintings one can assign all the
  appropriate colors and shapes, or omit some, or add some, or make them
  more numerous and larger, so too with names composed of letters.
  Assigning all appropriate elements produces fine (kala) images; adding
  or removing produces shoddy (poneras) ones.
textual_basis: >
  At 431c Socrates compares primary names to letters and then to paintings
  (zographēmata), stating that one can assign all the appropriate colors
  and shapes, or not all, but omit some, add others, and make them more
  numerous and larger. He then distinguishes two outcomes: the one who
  assigns everything appropriate produces fine letters and images; the one
  who adds or removes also produces letters and images, but shoddy ones.
limits: >
  This observation records the gradation-of-correctness dimension added to
  the painting-name analogy at 431c. It does not analyze whether this
  analogy commits Socrates to a mimetic theory of naming, nor does it
  assess the ontological implications of degrees of image-quality.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0263

greek_terms:
  - λόγοι

feature_family: definition_ladder
feature_id: feature_candidate_490
feature_label: definition_proposed
observation: >
  Socrates proposes a definition of speech (logos) as the composition
  (synthesis) of names (onomata) and verbs (rhemata), and uses this
  definition to argue that if names and verbs can be incorrectly
  assigned, then speeches can be false as well.
textual_basis: >
  At 431c Socrates states: "logoi gar pou, hōs egōimai, hē toutōn
  synthesis estin" — speeches are, I think, the composition of these
  (names and verbs). He asks Cratylus for confirmation, and Cratylus
  agrees ("houtō; kalōs gar moi dokeis legein"). The definition then
  functions as a premise for the argument that false speech is possible.
limits: >
  This observation records the explicit definition of logos as name-verb
  composition. It does not evaluate whether this definition is adequate
  or whether it is later revised in the dialogue. It also does not assess
  whether the definition is Socrates' own view or deployed for the sake
  of the argument with Cratylus.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates collapses Cratylus's appeal to "habit" (ethos) by equating it with "convention" (suntheke), forcing Cratylus to concede that his naturalist position reduces to the conventionalism he resists.
textual_basis: At 434e, Cratylus says we understand each other "through habit" (dia ge to ethos). Socrates immediately asks whether habit means anything different from convention/suntheke, and Cratylus concedes the equivalence across 434e-435a.
limits: This observation does not claim that Socrates proves conventionalism is correct; it only records the dialectical move that equates ethos with suntheke.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 435a, Socrates argues that if the lambda in skleron is unlike hardness yet still signifies, then "you yourself made an agreement with yourself" (autos sauto sunethou) and "correctness of the name becomes convention for you."
limits: This observation does not assess whether self-convention is the same as or different from social convention; it only records the refinement as stated at 435a.
```

```yaml
observation: Socrates concludes that both like and unlike letters (grammata) can reveal meaning, provided they obtain habit and convention, undermining the claim that only naturally resembling names can be correct.
textual_basis: At 435a, Socrates states that since both "like and unlike letters reveal" (deloi kai ta homoia kai ta anomoia grammata), once they have obtained "habit and convention" (ethous te kai sunthekes tuchonta), correctness of names must rest on convention.
limits: This observation does not claim that Socrates abandons natural correctness entirely; the passage ends mid-argument and the dialogue continues. It only records the concessive conclusion at this point.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates counters Cratylus's appeal to the internal consistency of names by saying that if the name-giver erred at the first step and then forced everything else to agree, internal consistency would prove nothing (436c-d)."
textual_basis: "At 436c, Cratylus offers the mutual agreement (symphtextbackslasha) of all names as proof that the name-giver did not err. Socrates replies that this is no defense (apologema), because if the first assumption was false, everything else could be forced into apparent agreement, like a diagram built on a small false premise (436c-d)."
limits: "This record notes the logical structure of the rebuttal. It does not claim Socrates rejects etymological method entirely, nor does it evaluate whether Cratylus's original consistency claim is well-founded."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 437d Cratylus says 'you see that the majority signified in that way.' Socrates responds with the vote-counting simile (psephous diarithmesometha) and asks whether correctness is decided by a majority of names. Cratylus concedes 'that is not reasonable' (oukoun eikos ge). Socrates then states at 437d-438a 'let us leave these things aside (tauta men ge autou easomen) and return again to where we left from.' He recalls a prior agreement that the name-giver must set names knowingly."
limits: "The observation records a procedural delimitation, not a refutation. Socrates does not prove that the majority method is false; he brackets it and returns to an earlier criterion."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 437d-438a Socrates says: 'just now in the previous discussion, if you remember, you said that the name-giver must necessarily set names knowingly (eidota tithesthai) for the things to which he set them. Does this still seem so to you or not?' Cratylus affirms: 'still.' Socrates then extends the question to the first name-giver (ton ta prota tithemenon) and asks from what names he learned or discovered, pressing on the consequence of Cratylus's own prior commitment."
limits: "The observation tracks the procedural move of recalling a prior concession, not whether the argument that follows from it succeeds."
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0273
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 437b-437c
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt

  text_sha256: e5c5b20ba7321df560b58022312df14bc8fcd640cd575f20d485f3098a858bab
greek_terms:
  - ἱστορία
  - ἵστησι
  - ῥοῦν
  - πιστὸν
  - μνήμη
  - μονή
  - φορά
  - ἁμαρτία
  - συμφορά
  - σύνεσις
  - ἐπιστήμη
  - ἀμαθία
  - ἀκολασία
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: etymology_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_2954
feature_label: etymology_by_phonetic_gloss
observation: At 437b-437c Socrates provides etymological analyses of several Greek terms in a sequence that treats them as signifying rest or stability rather than flux, explicitly reversing the earlier etymological direction that traced names to motion. The terms analysed include historia, piston, mneme, hamartia, symphora, amathia, and akolasia.
textual_basis: At 437b Socrates says that historia signals stopping the flow (histesi ton rhoun), that piston wholly signifies standing fast (histan), and that mneme indicates a staying (mone) in the soul rather than motion (phora). At 437b-c hamartia and symphora, when followed kata to onoma, appear the same as synesis and episteme and other serious names. At 437c amathia is analysed as the journey (poreia) of one going with a god (hama theo iontos), and akolasia as wholly a following (akolouthia) of things. Socrates then notes that names thought to belong to the worst things appear most similar to those for the finest, and that one would conclude the name-giver signified things as staying (menonta) rather than going or being carried (ionta oude pheromena).
limits: This observation records the etymological moves in this passage. It does not assess whether Socrates endorses these etymologies or is offering them as a counter-demonstration to the earlier flux-etymologies.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: At 437c Socrates says he thinks many other things could be found if one worked at it (pragmateuoito), from which one would conclude (oietheie an) that the name-giver signified things not as going or being carried but as staying. observation: Socrates explicitly ranks the hero etymology as easy and contrasts it with the etymology of "anthropoi," which he presents as more difficult.
```
```yaml
textual_basis: At 398e Socrates says "alla ou touto chalepon estin ennoesai, alla mallon to ton anthropon" (but this is not difficult to understand, but rather the case of humans [is difficult]). He marks an explicit difficulty gradient between two etymological targets.
limits: This records the difficulty contrast as a textual move; it does not claim that Socrates actually finds either etymology genuinely difficult or easy.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: turn_geometry
feature_id: feature_candidate_720
feature_label: explicit_procedure_control
observation: Hermogenes refuses to attempt the etymology of "anthropoi," explicitly stating he does not strain himself because he assumes Socrates will find the answer more readily.
textual_basis: At 398e Hermogenes replies that even if he might be able to discover something, "ou sunteino dia to hegeisthai se mallon heuresein he emauton" (I do not exert myself because I think you will find it more than I myself). This is a procedural move in which the respondent cedes the inquiry to Socrates.
limits: This records Hermogenes' procedural deferral; it does not assess whether his deference is sincere, ironic, or a dramatic device.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: |
  At 398c, Socrates explicitly links the daimon name to phronesis by way of a postulated older term, marked as a deleted word in the manuscript, arguing that the name registers the quality of being wise and good.
limits: |
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates argues that convention and custom must contribute to signification, because custom can signify by both likeness and unlikeness, and he treats Cratylus' silence as concession to the point."
textual_basis: "At 435b, Socrates says it would no longer be good to say that likeness alone is the means of disclosure, but rather custom, since it appears to disclose by both like and unlike. He then counts Cratylus' silence as agreement and says convention and custom must contribute to the disclosure of what speakers intend."
limits: "This records the local concession to convention inside the argument with Cratylus. It does not collapse Socrates' whole account into Hermogenes' earlier conventionalism."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates restates Cratylus' teaching claim as a claim about likeness and craft, then turns it into a question about whether learning beings through names is the best method or the only method."
textual_basis: "At 435e, Socrates infers that if one knows what a name is like, and it is like the thing, one will also know the thing, since the same craft concerns things like one another. He then asks what sort of teaching of beings this is and whether another method exists."
limits: "This records Socrates' reformulation and testing question. It does not yet give Socrates' answer to the method question."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 436b, Socrates asks Cratylus to consider someone seeking things by following names and inspecting what each name intends the thing to be. He says there is no small risk of deception, because the first name-setter set names according to how he supposed things to be; if that supposition was wrong, followers will be deceived."
limits: "This records the setup of Socrates' objection. It does not include Cratylus' full reply, which continues beyond this segment."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 439e, Socrates asks how something could be a thing if it is never in the same state. If it ever holds the same, it is not changing then; if it is always the same and the same thing, it cannot change or move without departing from its own idea or form."
limits: "This records the stated argument from sameness to non-motion. It does not settle the broader Heraclitean dispute by itself."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: "Socrates argues that total change would make knowledge impossible: the object would become different as the knower approaches, and knowledge itself must remain knowledge rather than shifting away from being knowledge."
textual_basis: "At 440a, Socrates says something changing into otherness as the knower approaches could not be known as what it is. He adds that it is not plausible to say knowledge exists if all things shift and nothing remains; if knowledge itself does not shift from being knowledge, it remains knowledge."
limits: "This records Socrates' epistemic objection to universal flux. It does not identify every premise needed for a complete theory of knowledge."
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
textual_basis: "At 440b, Socrates says that if the form of knowledge shifts into another form, it would not be knowledge, and if it always shifts, knowledge would never exist. feature_label: etymology_from_functional_predication
```
```yaml
observation: At 400a-400b Socrates derives the name for soul from the function of carrying and holding nature, after asking Hermogenes to assent that soul holds and carries the body and that mind and soul order and hold all things.
textual_basis: The passage moves from two assent questions about what holds and carries bodies and the cosmos to a proposed name for the power that carries and holds nature; Hermogenes then evaluates this derivation as more technical than the prior one.
limits: The record notes the local construction of a name from stated functions; it does not decide whether the derivation is philologically valid or whether Socrates endorses it without qualification.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml

  text_sha256: f2e052da9dcae42f026a2ecf001bf927fd6c00c718e04e52df59728b7034c0d3
greek_terms:
  - λόγος
  - Πάν
  - Ἑρμοῦ
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: myth_demarcation
feature_id: feature_candidate_4601
feature_label: mythic_figure_as_etymological_argument
observation: At 408c-408d Socrates identifies Pan as either identical to speech (logos) or as its brother, since Pan is the son of Hermes, and maps the double nature of speech (true and smooth above vs. false and rough below) onto the double form of Pan (smooth above, goat-like and rough below).
textual_basis: Socrates states that speech signifies the whole, is always in motion, and is double — true and false. The true is smooth and divine among the gods; the false is rough and tragic among humans. He then says Pan, the goat-herd son of Hermes, is two-formed in the same way, and that Pan is either speech itself or its brother, since both are children of Hermes (408c-408d).
limits: The observation records the identification and analogy only; it does not assess whether Socrates is serious or ironic in this etymological derivation.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation_id: obs_cratylus_0336
source_work: Cratylus
stephanus_span: 416b-416d
source_ref:
  source_path: raw/plato/greek/cratylus.txt
  stephanus_span: 416b-416d
  start_marker: 416b
  end_marker: 416d
  start_char: 64970
  end_char: 66249
  text_sha256: 025c0a4592f8bdee773d1dcf5b3e337ec31e9b8b8fcb80df427f00ab074aa857
greek_terms:
  - διάνοια
  - νοῦς
  - καλόν
  - φρόνησις
english_gloss: ""
feature_family: elenchus
feature_id: feature_candidate_071
feature_label: assent_chain
observation: >
  At 416c Socrates builds an assent chain establishing that the entity that
  assigned names to things is intelligence or thought, that naming and calling
  are the same activity as thought, and that whatever thought and intelligence
  produce is praiseworthy. At 416d he extends the chain to conclude that
  "kalon" is an epithet for the intelligence that produces things we call
  beautiful.
textual_basis: >
  Socrates asks whether the name-giver is intelligence of gods or humans or
  both (416c), then whether the thing that named and the thing that calls are
  the same as thought, eliciting assent at each step. At 416d he derives that
  since the naming power produces what is beautiful and is itself thought,
  "kalon" is correctly an epithet for phronesis (416d).
limits: >
  The observation records the structure of the assent chain; it does not
  evaluate the validity of the conclusion that the beautiful is intelligence.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
  naming power produces what is beautiful. The analogy establishes that a
  craft produces its characteristic product, then transfers this pattern to
  the activity of naming.
textual_basis: >
  Socrates asks whether the medical produces medical things and the carpentry
  craft produces carpentry things, Hermogenes agrees, and Socrates concludes
  "therefore the naming power produces beautiful things" (416d).

  naming is properly analogous to medicine and carpentry.
```

```yaml
feature_family: etymology_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_4606
feature_label: archaic_form_etymology
observation: At 418c Socrates states that women preserve the ancient form of speech most, and identifies phonetic substitutions used in modernizing words (iota replaced by epsilon or eta, delta replaced by zeta). He then cites two pairs of archaic and modern forms as evidence: the older form for "day" versus its modern form, and the older form for "yoke" versus its modern form at 418e.
textual_basis: The passage explicitly names women as preservers of the ancient tongue, lists the phonetic shifts, and gives the two word pairs with Hermogenes confirming each.
limits: The observation records the method and examples but does not assess whether the phonetic claims are linguistically accurate or whether Socrates endorses the method.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
feature_family: etymology_analysis
feature_id: feature_candidate_4607
feature_label: etymology_with_stated_rationale
observation: At 418d Socrates explains that the archaic word for "day" was so named because people were rejoicing and longing for light as it emerged from darkness, and that only the ancient form reveals this intended meaning, while the modern form has been altered beyond recognition.
textual_basis: Socrates states that the original name reveals the thought of its framer, then gives the specific rationale: people desired and welcomed light from darkness, and for that reason named the day as they did. Hermogenes agrees.
limits: The observation records the stated etymology and rationale but does not evaluate its linguistic validity or Socrates' sincerity.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: At 418e Socrates says that the word for "the necessary" or "the fitting," as currently spoken, signifies the opposite of what it should for all names concerning the good: being a form of the good, it appears to mean a bond and hindrance to motion, as though kin to the harmful.
textual_basis: Socrates explicitly states that the word signifies the opposite for names about the good, and identifies it as appearing to be a bond and impediment, a brother of the harmful. Hermogenes agrees strongly.
limits: The observation records the claim of name-reversal but does not resolve whether Socrates accepts this as a genuine problem for name-correctness or uses it dialectically.
review_status: accepted
```

```yaml
observation: >
  Socrates compares the proposed method of decomposing names into their phonetic
  elements to the procedure of those who study rhythms, who first divide the
  powers of the elements, then examine syllables, and only then proceed to
  rhythms (424b-c).
textual_basis: >
  At 424b-c Socrates asks whether, since the imitation of essence occurs through
  syllables and letters, it would be most correct to divide the elements first,
  just as those who study rhythms first divided the powers of the elements, then
  syllables, and then examined rhythms. Hermogenes assents.
limits: >
  The observation records the analogy and its structural parallel; it does not
  assess whether Socrates endorses the method as ultimately valid.
review_status: accepted
```
